<h4>You Still Got It</h4>


<p>I used to be like this more often, constantly doubting first if I had it at all, then later if I still got it, then finally if I’ll blow it.</p>
<p>Hitting post used to be absolutely terrifying, every time.</p>
<p>It’s no longer routine for me to freak out but I’m not zero percent like this now:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2090131877784891749">Dean W. Ball</a>: By the way if you are a young writer and not routinely freaking out about “whether you still got it” you aren’t doing it right. That anxiety is what drives you forward.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/anton_d_leicht/status/2090133228375928997">Anton Leicht</a>: i don’t know anyone whose writing I love whose work isn’t the product of defying ‘it’s so over, i’m washed, no one will ever read anything else i write again’ over and over again</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2090134249991004317">Dean W. Ball</a>: yes, Beethoven spoke of this too</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/natolambert/status/2090132399719878920">Nathan Lambert</a> (fixed it?): By the way if you are a writer and not routinely freaking out about “whether you still got it” you aren’t doing it right. That anxiety is what drives you forward.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2090133169420804283">Dean W. Ball</a>: Agreed! But I figure all the older writers know this :)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndyMasley/status/2090138897732755716">Andy Masley</a>: Literally every time I sit down with a new thing</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2090134685602951352">Dean W. Ball</a>: Almost literally every week I wake up and say to myself “everything good you’ve ever written is behind you, and it wasn’t even ever that good and now you have no more juice” and then I sit down to write</p></blockquote>
<p>What about me?</p>
<p>I worry that I’ve had or used all my good ideas, that I’ll be repeating myself.</p>
<p>I worry that I won’t recapture the magic that goes into a really great post.</p>
<p>I worry that I missed the key windows of time to say the important things.</p>
<p>I worry I’m getting too old and my brain is slowly not working right anymore.</p>
<p>I worry I’m going to start making big mistakes and look like an idiot.</p>
<p>My real anxiety these days comes not from ‘I don’t got it’ but from ‘I could blow it.’ It stems from the opportunity, from the stakes of the games I’m trying to play.</p>
<p>Whereas I guess if I ‘don’t have it’ then there’d really be nothing to sweat, you know?</p>
<p>The worries mostly apply to me for my ‘effort posts,’ where I’m trying to assemble a clear thesis and create something that will last. For roundups and weeklies, I don’t much worry about it. Those are more like ‘clocking in.’</p>
<p>I notice that most of the time, when I do really have something, I stop worrying.</p>
<p>I draw the parallel to being a competitor in Magic: The Gathering. I would worry I didn’t have it. When I was really clocked in I didn’t worry about not having it, I worried more about blowing the chance when I did have it. So it’s a similar pattern.</p>
<p>Is the anxiety actually useful? Does it drive you forward? To some extent yes. In order to write well you have to reinvent, to do something you haven’t done before, and you have to struggle and push through bad ideas and false starts and so on, and be intolerant of things that aren’t good enough or could be better.</p>
<p>But I don’t think it’s necessary, at least not for everyone. You just need something pushing you forward, some form of dread about not putting in every last bit of effort. You can be driven forward by opportunity or risk, rather than anxiety about ability, and I’m sure there are other similar tricks.</p>


<h4>How Scott Sumner Writes</h4>


<p>Scott Sumner looks at Virginia Woolf on writing advice, <a href="https://scottsumner.substack.com/p/virginia-woolf-on-writing">and offers a list of his own</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://scottsumner.substack.com/p/virginia-woolf-on-writing">Scott Sumner</a>:</p>
<blockquote>
<ol>
<li>Do a lot of reading—that’s where ideas come from. Read in all sorts of fields, even fields you might assume to be useless, such as philosophy. But also try to be an expert in at least one field.</li>
<li>Do a lot of writing, as writing helps one to think.</li>
<li>Think of blogging as a form of conversation. When you converse with people, you presumably are aware of your audience. That should also be true of blogging—think about who you are writing for.</li>
<li>Even a great novel or painting is a form of conversation, Artists are reacting to what other artists have done. A blogger just starting out should probably not be presenting grand theories of everything, rather one should start by reacting to what others are discussing.</li>
<li>People complained that I often “bury the lede”. So, I guess you shouldn’t do that. Give people an idea of where you are going if you want them to stick with the post—at least until you have enough of a reputation that they can trust you to go somewhere interesting.</li>
<li>Avoid a self-consciously literary style. Write as you’d speak.</li>
<li>Ask yourself: Is it possible I’ll regret this? How might this post be misinterpreted? You might want to set it aside for a day, then reread before posting. When I do so, I often end up cutting out things that might have brought me grief.</li>
<li>Personality flaws are exposed in blog posts, but writing also makes it easier to fix these flaws. Keep in mind that your critics are usually right (apart from trolls.) If a smart well-intentioned commenter says the post was unfair or mean-spirited, it usually is.</li>
<li>Words don’t just have literal meanings, they have&nbsp;<em>connotations</em>. The term ‘ignorant’ doesn’t mean stupid, but it has come to have that implication. Don’t think to yourself, “I should not have to tell readers that just because I claim a certain politician is an idiot, doesn’t mean that I view his supporters as stupid.”&nbsp;<strong><em>You do have to say that</em></strong>, even though logic would suggest it’s unnecessary.</li>
</ol>
<p>In other words, because people are stupid it is important to reassure them that you don’t view them as stupid.</p></blockquote>
<p>I especially endorse #2, #5 (I’m working on it), #6 and the core of #9. You do not have to say it, but you do have to either say it or accept that the connotation is there. There is no universal law saying you have to disclaim every connotation you don’t fully endorse, but you do want to leave them there on purpose.</p>


<h4>How Scott Alexander Writes</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/half-a-month-of-consolation-writing?utm_source=post-email-title&amp;publication_id=89120&amp;post_id=185242930&amp;utm_campaign=email-post-title&amp;isFreemail=true&amp;r=67wny&amp;triedRedirect=true&amp;utm_medium=email">Scott Alexander offers a bunch of writing advice</a>. Paraphrase summaries follow, then my reactions are nested.</p>
<ol>
<li>Avoid micro dishonesty. Don’t try to smuggle in your point, hide the weaknesses, go with the slightly misleading introduction, or pretend things turned out a way they didn’t. The readers will notice and it won’t work.
<ol>
<li>Agreed. Scott Sumner’s point on integrity is similar.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Avoid cliches, and treat them as opportunities to say a cool thing instead.
<ol>
<li>Agreed when it would be cliche in the bad way, but not in the good way.</li>
<li>That might sound unhelpful. What I mean is, don’t do the unfun straight man repetition thing, but playing around or using them like normal words is fine.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Follow writing rules until you understand the real underlying bad thing, which makes you good enough to get away with breaking the rule. Give yourself particular rules to follow that match what you need to fix.
<ol>
<li>Agreed, but don’t spend too long on that mountaintop.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Untangle your sentences, just say the thing, unless you have a good reason.
<ol>
<li>Agreed. Tangled is fine if and only if you know why you’re doing it.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>He’s against explainers.
<ol>
<li>My rule is, only write an explainer if you would often use it as a link.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>The five paragraph essay is awful but the basic idea behind it is good.
<ol>
<li>Yep. Tell ‘em what you’re gonna tell ‘em, tell ‘em, tell ‘em what you told ‘em.</li>
<li>This is one I have to keep working on. I keep reminding myself, at least one of the opening and the closing needs to contain the central takeaways.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Don’t write your blog about other blogs. Introduce new info or GTFO.
<ol>
<li>It has to be something that works if they’ve read your sources.</li>
<li>It also has to be something that works if they haven’t read your sources.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Write brilliantly about anything, or decently about what people care about.
<ol>
<li>Basically, yeah.</li>
<li>As Scott says, most success will be from option two. Option one is harder.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>[A parable that one can interpret a lot of different ways.]</li>
<li>There are tons of effort posts out there waiting to be written.
<ol>
<li>I mean, sure.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>The true blogger finds unexpected opportunities to start pointless but delightful conflicts.
<ol>
<li>Strongly agree. Go forth and find them. It’s like abstract mathematics, in that you think it’s all pointless, and it works better if you don’t worry about the point, but it then turns out there was a point after all.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>The point of poetry is that restrictions breed creativity, so you can strike gold.
<ol>
<li>Maybe. I dunno. I don’t really get it.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>You need to always buy enough runway to keep reader attention. Start strong.
<ol>
<li>Strongly agree, in most situations.</li>
<li>Notice that sometimes you actually have quite a lot of runway. Use it.</li>
<li>Also notice that once you’re profitable, you can keep going indefinitely.</li>
<li>Well, kinda. I test that theory a lot.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>You need conflict and mystery but it can be pretty much anything.
<ol>
<li>I think of it as interest or curiosity. Probably mostly the same idea.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Give readers things they can’t trivially generate from a prompt.
<ol>
<li>Whenever possible, this is a good idea. Need it somewhere.</li>
<li>But also, they mostly won’t bother to run the prompt.</li>
<li>Often you need it to have been said as a reference point.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Just say the thing you want to say.
<ol>
<li>Yep.</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>


<h4>How Jasmine Sun Writes</h4>


<p>She does her homework.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://jasmi.news/p/writing-2025">Jasmine Sun</a>: When you become a writer, here is one of the first things you will discover: with about a month of dedicated effort, you can go from knowing nothing about a topic to being able to present fluently and converse with experts. The beginning will be painful.</p>
<p>You will sound stupid for your first ten conversations, maybe fifty.</p>
<p>At first jargon will look like gibberish, then it will be faintly recognizable, and then one day intelligible, like fog clearing from the hills; with every additional source you read, your story will reveal itself in greater fidelity and scope. “If you do everything, you’ll win,” as Robert Caro says.</p>
<p>There is a comforting<em> if-then</em> determinism to Caro’s advice: research persistence is rewarded, success is guaranteed. You chip away at your hypothesis until something more interesting is revealed: an essay is a failure if you go in with an outline and it emerges the same shape. The world is both unimaginably intricate and more knowable than it looks.</p></blockquote>
<p>Fifty conversations or interviews about a topic seems like a lifetime to me. That’s a very different alien mode of being and way of learning. I don’t know that I’ve ever even done two. But Jasmine is doing a very different kind of investigative or exploratory journalism, based largely on interviews. Whereas I depend on learning from what is written.</p>
<p>It seems so weird to impose on people and ask them to be interviewed, especially when Claude is right there, even though I get asked and I often say yes. I should go to the sources more often.</p>
<p>Jasmine is constantly taking notes about everything. On the margin that has to be right, yet I find myself unmotivated to do it.</p>
<p>Another alien thing to me is a work in progress ‘living in your head’ like this:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://jasmi.news/p/writing-2025">Jasmine Sun</a>: At some point in every writing project, it stops living in the Google Doc and starts living in your mind. Everything else you see and hear gets filtered through the frame of the essay.</p></blockquote>
<p>To me, to the extent work lives in my head, that is before you open the doc, not during or after. There are also a bunch more things she talks about <a href="https://jasmi.news/p/writing-2025">in the full essay.</a></p>


<h4>How Various Famous Writers Write</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.theguardian.com/books/2010/feb/20/ten-rules-for-writing-fiction-part-one">Useful pieces of writing advice from several writers</a>, from 2010.</p>
<p>Elmore Leonard is all about what stupid things not to do: Never open with weather, avoid prologues, always use “said” for dialogue without adverbs, almost no exclamation points, no ‘suddenly’ or ‘all hell broke loose,’ minimal regional dialect, no detailed character descriptions, avoid long place and thing details, leave out things readers skip. If it sounds like writing, rewrite it.</p>
<p>Diana Athill says read it aloud to yourself, have no inessential words, be highly suspicious of your darlings.</p>
<p>Margaret Atwood focuses on physical tools like pencils and a thesaurus that I would say you don’t need anymore. She also steps back to earlier points if something went awry, does back exercises and hold the reader’s attention and has others look with fresh eyes.</p>
<p>There’s a bunch more. The biggest takeaway is they have some overlaps but are all different, and often very personal things that won’t generalize. The only commonality is ‘write.’</p>


<h4>How Nabeel Qureshi Defines Great Writing</h4>


<p><a href="https://nabeelqu.substack.com/p/what-makes-art-great?utm_source=post-email-title&amp;publication_id=107423&amp;post_id=196253277&amp;utm_campaign=email-post-title&amp;isFreemail=true&amp;r=3o9&amp;triedRedirect=true&amp;utm_medium=email">What makes text into great art</a>? Nabeel Qureshi says good text is surprising and also it echoes over layers doing multiple things at once, it makes you think, it changes what it means as you read it. Great writing contains themes and layers most readers never notice. It has depth that is impossible to paraphrase, contains mysteries and is open to different interpretations.</p>
<p>I would say these are some of the things that make art great, and yes for now we do not know how to get AI to do this. Then again, I don’t know how to create ‘great art’ either, and have yet to produce what I consider even a short passable work of fiction or poetry.</p>


<h4>Quickly, There’s No Time</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DiiLDbHxbrHLAyXaq/why-people-like-your-quick-bullshit-takes-better-than-your">Eukaryote points out that quick take posts are often about interesting things</a> that require little context. This can be a lot more important than all the details and editing you pour into your effort posts.</p>
<p>In particular I’d note that having a great hook is a lot of what gets one to go viral and get posted to places like Hacker News, and that virality is how you get your hits until you’ve built up a big subscription base.</p>
<p>You can of course do even better by producing the cool concept post and then editing it within an inch of its life, as in ‘taking the time to write a shorter letter.’</p>
<p>Some, but far from all, of my historically successful posts did that.</p>


<h4>If At First</h4>


<p>Is it better to write quickly and revise, or write slowly and get it right the first time?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/gabydvj/status/2019519667988230653">g a b y</a>: the most liberating thing in the world is realizing you can just write it bad. you can write it bad and make it good later. does everyone know about this??????</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/NateSilver538/status/2019630605630140488">Nate Silver</a>: I write like shit sometimes but actually bad advice, writing bad and revising takes longer than chilling out and writing well once you’re good and ready.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KyleBown/status/2019706134823932410">Kyle Bown</a>: I think it depends greatly on the KIND of writing you’re doing. I find if I’m writing a script, I need to just get the first draft out. There’s so many moving pieces to telling a story in that format that editing is necessary. An article or think piece is better to do it the first time.</p></blockquote>
<p>Here is another case of Nate Silver pushing back against ‘write the first draft quick.’</p>
<p>If you are struggling to write something, could it be you don’t know what you think? That is indeed my experience.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/PaulSaxMD/status/2089298379436114124">Paul Sax</a>: Excellent advice for avoiding writer’s block. Just get the draft down as fast as possible. Revise later.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/NateSilver538/status/2089545247139254537">Nate Silver</a>: Not meaning to be critical. This will work great for some people. I just think there’s very wide variation among writers about whether you do your “thinking on the page” vs. “thinking in your head”.</p>
<p>I’m on the extreme opposite end where like the first two drafts are in my head, maybe with a little bit of outlining or a few snippets written down (certainly for longer pieces). Not inherently good or bad, but that’s how my brain works for whatever reason.</p>
<p>So for me, when I’m struggling to write, it’s often because I haven’t done enough reading or given myself enough time to let ideas marinate. Just “getting something down on the page” can sometimes make that worse especially if you grow attached to your copy.</p></blockquote>
<p>I largely think on the page, as I write. Which means I often start writing before I know what I want to say. It also means I would never write a passage in order to have an early version, or move on from a paragraph that I know is not good enough. If it is not good enough, I am not done thinking. I am not ready to move on.</p>
<p>I also think that part of why my process works so quickly is that I don’t train myself that it is okay to write these ‘vomit drafts.’ If you’re willing to do that, you’re committing to rewrites.</p>
<p>Then again, I never write fiction. If I wrote fiction, I could totally see doing what famed Simpsons writer Swartzwelder says to do, writing filler dialogue and jokes to have a first draft, so you know what you are looking at, and then fill in from there. Maybe that’s a good idea.</p>
<p>It’s fine to write badly, so long as you notice, as an exercise, but then you need to throw most of it out and start over. You can keep actively good bits.</p>
<p>The key difference I have is, for my purposes writing nonfiction, I find that trying to revise bad writing is way harder than writing well in the first place. If I realize something is bad I’m inclined to entirely start over. In fiction perhaps it does not work that way? I’m not sure.</p>
<p>None of this gets you out of editing. Editing starts with good and makes it great.</p>


<h4>Writers Have A Harder Time Influencing, But It Can Still Be Done</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.noahpinion.blog/p/does-anything-i-write-matter-anymore?utm_source=post-email-title&amp;publication_id=35345&amp;post_id=202656876&amp;utm_campaign=email-post-title&amp;isFreemail=false&amp;r=6g77v&amp;triedRedirect=true&amp;utm_medium=email">Noah Smith is worried that bloggers are losing influence</a>.</p>
<p>Part of this is that we are in a populist era, when the right is led by Trump who acts on instincts built from TV and real estate and zero sum games, and listens to whoever talked to him last, and the left is largely insular and only listens inside its echo chamber. So you can’t much influence either.</p>
<p>I would respond that yes this makes it harder, but you can still influence both.</p>
<p>Dean Ball went straight to the White House and has had strong influence on policy. When the time comes to grope around for those instincts, or for whoever talks to Trump last to talk to him last, someone is still puppeteering that, to a large degree. I’ve had various doors opened for me as well.</p>
<p>You can still drive the online conversation. There are those in the administration very aware of sentiments on Substack and Twitter. Keep posting. But do watch framing. You can turn the administration against you if you provoke the wrong adversarial frame.</p>
<p>On the left, you have the moderates who listen to the intellectuals more than ever, and no they don’t purely fold to the left. As for the harder left like Mamdani, you still see him for example knowing his YIMBY facts. These people do sometimes listen. Other times not so much directly, and absolutely they can be off in their own world, but part of the job is to make sure that world doesn’t end up in charge. So there’s at least that.</p>


<h4>It’s Not (Only) The Incentives, It’s (Also) You</h4>


<p>Smith’s second concern is that Substack is a newsletter platform that rewards those who maximize subscribers, so you end up isolated and not in dialogue with other bloggers and thinkers. There’s no <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Letters">republic of letters</a>.</p>
<p>My response is a mix of ‘it’s not the incentives, it’s you’ plus skill issue. Noah Smith has over 300k subscribers. He doesn’t have to be maximizing revenue. I have a lot less subscribers, largely because I don’t maximize, because I recognize that the impact is on a power law, by bringing key things to the key people who will act on that.</p>
<p>I do worry about the ‘silo effect,’ where you do better if you stay in your lane. I want to write about games more, or other fun things, but that gets punished. I pay some price for the tangents I do insist upon.</p>
<p>I find the silo problem even worse on Twitter or other social media than Substack. If you’re giving me 50% Tweets I want and 50% Tweets I don’t want, then that’s no good, because the cost of evaluating a Tweet is high relative to the cost of full consumption. Whereas if you give me 50% articles or books I want, and 50% that I don’t, that’s great. You can totally spam my inbox with the other half, because I spend one minute discarding the bad things and 1 hour consuming the good thing. It’s only a problem if the bad things fool you into investing a lot of time.</p>
<p>Smith’s third concern is that AI is hurting our attention spans, causing more skimming versus less reading, and generating a lot of its own posts. Here I worry less.</p>
<p>I strongly disagree with him that the AI-written articles are already good. You can use Claude to generate serviceable text if the goal is an infodump and you also do a bunch of work, if you want. It’s at best serviceable and like Noah’s example would likely show up at ~50% AI on Pangram. At least for now. Such text plays somewhat better to audiences who don’t yet have much AI writing exposure.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/CharlesD353/status/2004519559617974408">Matt Yglesias notes he took a pay cut and gave up his Bloomberg Opinion column to write in The New York Times</a> in order to reach the audience only NYT can provide. As someone who did successfully get one piece into NYT, I can say the same except at even higher abstraction. I wrote that article in order to write future articles that would then get a key message across. The other advantage is I had a really great editor. It was a lot of work but also a joy to have someone who cared about making my work the best it can be.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/lulumeservey/status/1999154566290416042">Lulu Cheng Meservey charts new sales after various exposures</a>. National outlets did little, substacks did relatively a lot.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rA6pqn6kz8NvHyznT/hooj9a6kew8dlle6mjf0" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>This matched my own experience. I got into The New York Times once and if I showed you my subscriber graph you wouldn’t be able to notice that date at all. That doesn’t mean it isn’t impactful in other ways, especially in reaching a lot of otherwise hard to reach people who matter.</p>


<h4>Beware The Fetish of the Desk</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/PerellClips/status/2090226674965073970">I am heavily on the pragmatist side in terms of your setup</a>. You should indeed as Robert McFarlane says ‘beware the fetish of the desk’ and not worry about getting the details right or keeping things straight or what not. If you find clutter is bothering you, fix it. If you don’t like your pencil, get a better pencil, and so on.</p>
<p>The exception is your computer and monitor setup. Invest in that, whether or not you are a writer. You want at least two monitors, with the central one being large. I use three 30-inch monitors, all three are fully used at all times, and if I did it over again I would have four but have decided it isn’t worth redoing everything to fix that.</p>
<p>I find that when forced to write without my setup, I am at least ~50% less productive. I’ve given myself a rule that I don’t try to write elsewhere, unless I am going to be in a place for an extended period. If so I try damn hard to get at least one good monitor.</p>
<p>Then again, there are plenty of writers who don’t need secondary screens, and are perfectly happy to write on laptops. For some forms I think this is fine, for others not.</p>


<h4>How Orson Scott Card Writes</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/orsonscottcard/status/2036803795087204768">Orson Scott Card on how he expanded a short story into the full book Ender’s Game in a matter of weeks</a>. He says he did almost no prep for the book, because his prep was his childhood, which he transferred directly onto Ender, and things like a trilogy of books about the Army of the Potomac which mapped onto the other commanders at battle school.</p>
<p>This resonated with me. I spent a long time thinking about related ideas, but once I decided to write the <a href="https://thezvi.wordpress.com/2020/05/23/mazes-sequence-summary/">Moral Mazes</a> sequence I mostly wrote it within a week, and a lot of my other key works that have held up well were written quickly.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/orsonscottcard/status/2046702294406680751">Orson Scott Card also explains that when you get rejected as a writer</a>, you should treat that the same way game designers treat such feedback. When they say something is wrong, they’re right, and they can often point towards it. When they tell you how to <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yo3uxqwTxk0&amp;pp=ygUPZml4IGl0IHNubCBza2l0">fix it</a>, you can mostly ignore them, and should figure out yourself how to <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yo3uxqwTxk0&amp;pp=ygUPZml4IGl0IHNubCBza2l0">fix it</a>.</p>
<p>Another note is that when you are rejected, the editor usually won’t even know why they are rejecting you, so they definitely can’t tell you the reason. Very true.</p>
<p>The last note is that editors are dramatically different before and after you sign a contract. Before you sign a contract, they’re usually not concentrating attention enough to be general intelligences and don’t know much. After you sign a contract, now they have a stake in the outcome, and if they are good at their job then they’re great and invaluable. My pitching experience has been extremely frustrating, but every time an editor actually focused on improving the writing it has been great.</p>


<h4>Doing The Math Is Fun And Supererogatory</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/drethelin/status/2038034107217952858">Roach-works notes that if you are writing speculative fiction</a> and you get detailed, some of your readers will point out your details are wrong and be upset about this. So Roach’s advice is to not give specifics. The city is old, the sword is heavy.</p>
<p>To which Misha correctly responds, screw the haters, you can ignore them.</p>
<p>Misha is correct, if you are willing and able. It is much better to make some mistakes than to keep everything vague. However, keeping everything vague is also totally an option, if you do not want to do the research.</p>
<p>You can make mistakes. You don’t want to be making glaringly obvious mistakes, or have your economic values be off by orders of magnitude such that the whole thing makes no sense. And you may not need the details at all. If you want to tell a story that fundamentally doesn’t physically work, then it’s time for that heavy sword.</p>


<h4>Brevity is the Soul of Wit</h4>


<p>How long is too long? Asking for a friend (named Dean Ball).</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2036260243441680886">Dean W. Ball</a>: I just wrote a 5,300 word essay. Should I yeet it out as one long essay or split it into two parts? A normal essay on Hyperdimensional is 2.5-3k words. The risk to two parts is neither half can sustain itself without the other; the risk to one is obviously overwhelming readers.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rA6pqn6kz8NvHyznT/yosyktfzx6lebruieu9n" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/patio11/status/2036426803758174463">Patrick McKenzie</a>: The people who run the world mostly read, and people who read are not intimidated by pieces at their natural length. If the natural length is 5k words, ship 5k words.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/BenShindel/status/2036265111552200979">Ben</a>: The public yearns for longpost</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2036265407019966552">Dean W. Ball</a>: I’m kinda shocked</p></blockquote>
<p>My take for posts that have a thesis, rather than being roundups:</p>
<p>You should only ever split an article into two small parts in places where the parts can stand on their own. If no one would ever want to link to or read Part 1 without Part 2, or Part 2 without Part 1, then you want to post it all at once, no matter the length.</p>
<p>That assumes you are on the internet, so you face no physical limitations. Except the Gmail clipping at ~102KB, but that ship sailed for me a while ago.</p>
<p>The reason The Sequences work so well is that while they form one large construction, you can link to or read each individual post on its own.</p>
<p>If you have a full book of content, then you have to split it into chapters, even if the chapters don’t stand on their own, but I would not consider that below 10k words, and would only seriously consider it around 20k, even if your normal length is 2k.</p>
<p>For roundups, I think it’s fine to split sooner, especially if that gives the sections better common themes. My new goal is non-AI roundups topping out around 5k.</p>
<p>So, while noting that everyone’s process is different and you need to find what works for you via experimentation, we’d better end here before we hit that mark.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rA6pqn6kz8NvHyznT/on-writing-3#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The Forkmakers](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NjLQf3QC4q4DD67kD/the-forkmakers)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Imagine our civilization fell tomorrow. What would our descendants think of us? What would they know about the 21st century?</span></p><p><span>They would know surprisingly little about our greatest material triumphs. Our civilization’s favorite building materials aren’t made to last. Reinforced concrete only lasts a century; asphalt far less. Most of what we make out of steel will turn into a brownish oxidized dust in a few decades.</span></p><p><span>Information is even worse. The ancient Mesopotamians did their writing on clay tablets. Our knowledge is stored on hard drives, which die in a few years, or acidic paper, which dies in decades.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NjLQf3QC4q4DD67kD/zo39y37spwzyqlelrgkz" /><figcaption><p><span>Which receipt do you think will last longer?</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>We do create things that will last. Glass (especially its shatter-resistant varieties), ceramics, stainless steel. 1000 years after the fall of our civilization, we will be known for one thing above all others: cutlery. Our heirs call us the Forkmakers.</span></p><h3><span>What Survives a Thousand Years</span></h3><p><span>Our civilization is large and powerful. We will leave lots of relics for the post-apocalypse. Coins, tires, aluminum cans. Vast landfills of disposable diapers. But all of that is useless. The most durable thing we make that our successors actually want to use is our silverware.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NjLQf3QC4q4DD67kD/npub9ggrcxvm1r0xxg8n" /><p><span>Cutlery is metal - thick metal. Anything designed to last a hundred runs in the dishwasher can withstand the elements. And we’ve made a lot of it. In this post-apocalyptic world, even the poorest peasant can afford to eat with a stainless steel fork.</span></p><p><span>The situation with plates is more complicated. </span><span>Few natural processes can destroy them.</span><span> But when the Forkmakers’ civilization died, they left many of their plates in wooden cupboards off the ground. The few survivors didn’t really prioritize safely removing the plates before the cupboards rotted, so billions of ceramic plates shattered in those dark decades. But somewhere like the Bay Area, which had a large and rich Forkmaker population, most middle-class families still have the ancestral Fiestaware.</span></p><p><span>And it’s fortunate they have the cookware of a more advanced civilization, because one of their plentiful foods - acorns - is difficult to prepare. But with enough resourcefulness and inherited forks, the people of the Bay Area enjoy a diet of acorns, wild oats, grapes and figs. They call themselves Oaklanders.</span></p><p><span>What about the rest of our metalwork? While stainless steel objects last forever, most other forms of steel rust away. The chassis of a car or the springs inside a mattress will be red dust before the first century is over.</span></p><p><span>Aluminum will be more interesting. After iron and steel, it’s the most important metal in the modern world.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhver4ezccdn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhver4ezccdn">[1]</a></sup></span><span> It’s corrosion-resistant. Early scavengers looking for finished goods they can use will ignore decaying engine blocks and crushed soda cans in favor of our forks and knives. But as they move up the tech tree, the Oaklanders will start to see aluminum as a useful raw material. Aluminum melts more easily than bronze, and can be cast just as easily.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6ehr7khatk4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6ehr7khatk4">[2]</a></sup></span><span> During humanity’s first rise, we didn’t learn to extract aluminum from ore until the 1880s. But with millions of tons of the stuff already extracted, the Oaklanders will have an Aluminum Age before their Bronze Age. For a shining moment, before easy aluminum reserves are exhausted, they could appreciate their ancestors for something besides forks.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NjLQf3QC4q4DD67kD/cbmsveigmwqg27wsgkt3" /><figcaption><p><span>Aluminum isn’t hard enough to make a sword. But I love the idea of future warriors carrying the lightweight metal shields.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>A thousand years from now, they still have some of our glass.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzufsng5a6i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzufsng5a6i">[3]</a></sup></span><span> Our phone screens survive, long after the rest of the phone is gone. They might be fun to play with; I imagine my distant descendants hunched over the glass screen ignoring their parents.</span></p><p><span>Windows and lightbulbs have mostly been smashed: either by time or by barbarian raiders from San Jose. Tempered glass - most of the windows of a car - is cooled non-uniformly, putting it under a constant pattern of stress and strain. This means if a car window breaks, the break spreads, turning it into gravel.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NjLQf3QC4q4DD67kD/bd7rfuplvrzdasfpoolw" /><figcaption><p><span>The outer layers are under compression, meaning a tiny nick will get sealed up. But the strong internal forces mean that when the glass fails, it fails spectacularly. Tap a car window or shower door hard enough and the whole thing shatters. Tempered glass is neat!</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>But tempered glass is </span><i><em>tough. </em></i><span>The future Bay Area has hundreds of thousands of car windows. Oaklander architecture allows for plenty of natural light, shining through glass from another civilization.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NjLQf3QC4q4DD67kD/juvsdemasiv1opuj61wq" /><p><span>Interestingly, while conventional wisdom holds that the Forkmakers are responsible for glass, natural philosophers have long disagreed. They rolled their eyes at a ‘Forkmaker of the Gaps’ who can be invoked to explain any unexplained phenomenon. Eventually, large window quarries are discovered in what was once Southern California. Each one corresponds to a large concentration of forks. The scientific consensus on what the Forkmakers could do shifts.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NjLQf3QC4q4DD67kD/utwt9xssafbkw3xstxgi" /><figcaption><p><span>Future window quarry</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>So much for metal and glass. What about plastic? No individual piece of plastic will see as much usage as our forks and plates; plastic degrades too fast when exposed to UV light. But anyone digging through our cities will find literal tons of the stuff: mysterious shapeable substances that nobody knows how to replicate, that aren’t made by any known processes. Still, if you aren’t superstitious, you can just go into an old city or landfill and get more. There are countless plastics, but the Forkmakers conveniently stamped a little code on each of them. The plasticsmith is one of the most learned people in the village. He knows which plastics mix with what, and what their properties will be.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NjLQf3QC4q4DD67kD/jfwuexebj8dxtoxvtwlp" /><figcaption><p><span>Ever spend ten minutes trying to remember whether plastic wrapping goes in the recycling bin? People will still be doing that in a thousand years.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Plastic can be melted down by even a primitive culture, and reforged into something new. It’s lightweight, waterproof, and cheap. In the Bay Area, that mostly means cups, jugs, and buckets. But in rainier climates, homes will have plastic roofs.</span></p><p><span>It’s interesting to compare the roles of plastic and aluminum. Both are lightweight substances that are easy to work with. Both need advanced technology to produce, but were thrown far back in the tech tree by the fallen Forkmakers. Plastic and aluminum share many applications. Plastic actually melts at a lower temperature, and requires much less physical infrastructure to work with. But it comes in countless complicated varieties. And the fumes it gives off have the unfortunate tendency to kill off all but the most carefully tutored plasticsmiths before they reach the point of mastery. Today there are about five tons of plastic for every ton of aluminum, but the most easily accessible reserves will be degraded by UV radiation in a few decades. Whether the Plastic Age comes before or after the Aluminum Age depends on the details of the recovery: how quickly do the Oaklanders amass technological know-how versus material capital?</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NjLQf3QC4q4DD67kD/hu3uomtjpzhahgazdps2" /><figcaption><p><span>Did you know that Forkmaker action figures were brightly colored? We only think of them as white because they got bleached by sunlight before they were buried.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h3><span>The Words of the Forkmakers</span></h3><p><span>A thousand years from now, the Oaklanders are a powerful civilization. Recycling ancient plastic and aluminum is an important part of their economy, and they’ve even learned to work with steel, making our forks more valuable than ever.</span></p><p><span>But I don’t want to be remembered for my forks. Forkmaker society produced so much information: photographs, essays, newspapers, scientific journals. Unfortunately, the next thousand years aren’t going to be kind to the things we write on.</span></p><p><span>Digital information is toast. Even if this laptop was somehow preserved from the elements, even if someone could find electricity in the post-apocalypse, information on an SSD only lasts decades. CDs might last a little longer. But anything lasting a millennium is a deliberate stunt by an archivist, not a widespread storage technique that just happens to be millennium-rated. There are a </span><a href="https://www.memory-of-mankind.com/1000books/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>thousand books</span></a><span> carved in ceramic buried in a salt mine in Austria, and a couple thousand </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosetta_Project" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Rosetta disks </span></a><span>(unreadable unless you have a microscope).</span></p><p><span>So digital is out. I’ve written millions of words in my life (and been the subject of trillions of bytes of espionage by tech companies). All of that is gone.</span></p><p><span>I’ve also been written about in a newspaper. But newspapers don’t last a thousand years. Paper, in general, doesn’t really last. It’s biodegradable and slightly acidic. Certain kinds of mites can eat it, water and fire both destroy it.</span></p><p><span>But we do have examples of paper-like things lasting a thousand years with nobody to take care of them. There are hundreds of thousands of pieces of papyrus that survived twice that long in the hot, dry desert of inland Egypt. There’s Roman writing on pressed wood as far north as England. There are thousand-year-old sutras written on paper - actual paper! - in a cave in China.</span></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 62.110555135951664%;"><img alt="Pelliot_hébreu_1.jpg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787620668/lexical_client_uploads/w8oashdknn2smn5lhix8.jpg" /><figcaption><p><span>The Mogao Caves in Western China were in use for longer than the Library of Alexandria and the Library of Congress put together. This manuscript was forgotten for almost a thousand years before it was dug up around 1900. And yes, that text from Medieval China is written in Hebrew. </span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>You can’t exactly curl up at night and read the Oxyrhynchus papyri: they’re too brittle. In the course of researching this piece, I learned that there are more types of paper than I had ever imagined, and their fates are at least as varied as the fates of our windows. Roughly speaking, paperbacks are all doomed. Hardcover books are printed on acid-free paper not unlike ancient papyrus. It’ll dry out, but if you take it to a humid environment, it might become bendable again. Glossy stock (think nice magazines) survives the best of all of these if you keep it dry. If it ever gets wet, though, mold will devour it, and it’s hard to stay dry for a thousand years anywhere on this planet.</span></p><p><span>What does it take for a library to last a millennium? It can’t be anywhere humid. Bad news, since we Forkmakers do most of our building on coasts. Nowhere with precipitation. Nowhere that will be inhabited at any point in the future: we humans have a nasty habit of burning each other’s cities to the ground.</span></p><p><span>But we Forkmakers live in many places. There are a hundred thousand libraries in the continental US,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkp02zt0b41"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkp02zt0b41">[4]</a></sup></span><span> ranging from elementary school collections to the Library of Congress. They’re built in every architectural style imaginable: many from the doomed mix of concrete and steel, some from wood or glass, but some from brick or stone. Many have archival stacks buried in basements. And, most important, some are in places that nobody- not a barbarian warlord, not a hunter-gatherer looking for kindling, not even a rat or termite, would ever think to venture. I’m talking about Las Vegas.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefoz9ym3xkc4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnoz9ym3xkc4">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>The Lied Library at UNLV is perfect for preservation. The books are packed into metal bins the size of refrigerators. In our time, patrons can ask a robot to retrieve any title they wish. But fifty years from now, the bins will be buried under four stories of rubble. Many will be smashed when the building falls. But many will survive.</span></p><p><span>Imagine the excitement when an archeologist, digging in Las Vegas, discovers a university library. Millions upon millions of pages, more than she could read in a lifetime. She reaches out to touch one, and it crumbles to dust. But the Oaklanders are resourceful. Our heroine realizes that if they take the pages back to California, some become readable.</span></p><p><span>Well, readable is a generous term. In the time of the Forkmakers, there were only two major languages in North America: English and Spanish. A truly unprecedented amount of linguistic uniformity. After a thousand years, the Spanglish our Oaklander archeologist speaks is similar enough to English that if she had a modern recording, she could recognize the words, if not the grammar. Unfortunately, she doesn’t have a recording; she has writing. And English is a very hard language to read.</span></p><p><span>The breakthrough comes when she finds two different Forkmaker translations of the Bible. The Oaklanders have Bibles of their own and are able to decipher the strange writings of their forebears.</span></p><p><span>Understanding the text is the work of decades. The books are organized by size, fiction next to science next to self-help. There are scholarly debates about whether Elon Musk was a real person. There’s a scientific boom as Oaklander scholars work their way through the university library. But the greatest mystery of all is never answered: why do so few of the books talk about how to make a fork?</span></p><img alt="50 X Retro Salad / Dessert Forks - Mid Century - Mix + Match - Vintage  Stainless Set - Bulk Lot - Etsy" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NjLQf3QC4q4DD67kD/dakynfbggcwwlksnqu5q" /><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhver4ezccdn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhver4ezccdn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Sorry, copper, no hard feelings</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6ehr7khatk4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6ehr7khatk4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>At least if you alloy it with silicon, which the Forkmakers helpfully did.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzufsng5a6i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzufsng5a6i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There’s a pernicious rumor going around that glass is a slow-flowing liquid. People sometimes justify this by pointing to old windows and noticing they’re uneven. By this logic, Roman glasswork couldn’t possibly have its </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_glass#/media/File:Munich_Cup_Diatretum_22102016_1.jpg" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>incredible detail</span></a><span>, and Neolithic </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obsidian#/media/File:Arrowhead.jpg" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>obsidian arrowheads</span></a><span> would be puddles by now.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkp02zt0b41"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkp02zt0b41">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>After a great deal of debate with Claude, I conceded that Canadian and Alaskan libraries are getting swallowed by fire- either natural or man-made- well before the millennium mark.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnoz9ym3xkc4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefoz9ym3xkc4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The city was abandoned soon after the collapse, but its location was remembered by desert nomads for centuries. A thousand years from now, the Hoover Dam is still a tourist attraction; the Las Vegas ruins are for serious historians.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NjLQf3QC4q4DD67kD/the-forkmakers#comments">Discuss</a>

### [PSA: We can do better](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wiFv6LguphSxkzAnb/psa-we-can-do-better)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>tl;dr: people should understand and think hard about the problems they work on.</span></i></p><p><span>We’ve observed that those who work in AI safety (ourselves included) often rely on concerning heuristics when choosing what to work on. Running a conference is probably good, doing pragmatic alignment research might be good, and as long as such objectives don’t breach our internal models of what could contribute to reducing x-risk, these things are “what should be done”. But using such vibesy thought processes don’t always produce “actually impactful work” that would beat a prospective counterfactual. We wrote this post to share our observations and figure out what we should be doing instead.</span></p><h2><b><span>People don’t know what they’re working on</span></b></h2><p><span>AI safety is </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/NrYkhgh6ZGrGtxxnc/talent-constraints-in-ai-safety-what-we-know-and-what-we-don"><span>talent constrained</span></a><span>. However, simply inflating the field doesn’t solve our bottleneck; rather, we need more people who </span><b><span>understand the core arguments of AI safety</span></b><span>. You can’t determine how to meaningfully contribute to AI safety without deeply knowing the problem you are trying to solve. Many newer people (us included!) rush into research, fellowships, and the like without building the context necessary for navigating the field.</span></p><h2><b><span>Agency-maxxing is not always good</span></b></h2><p><span>Moving fast is good. Moving </span><i><span>too</span></i><span> fast leads to poor ToC and sloppily executing projects. Many people working in AI safety seem to downweight spending time thinking about the actual problem they are trying to solve and backchaining from it, in favor of moving as fast as possible to get things done quick and dirty. While this enables faster execution and increases the volume of work being done, it doesn’t always move the needle on effectively reducing x-risk.</span></p><h2><b><span>The problem with force multipliers</span></b></h2><p><span>We categorize ToCs that focus on enhancing the impact of others as </span><b><span>force multipliers</span></b><span> (think upskilling, infrastructure work). There’s strong incentives to become a force multiplier: enhancing the impact of others through auxiliary work is (arguably) higher ROI and often more approachable than “directly” working on AI safety.</span></p><p><span>However, when </span><i><span>everyone</span></i><span> becomes a force multiplier, no one ends up directly working on the problems we actually care about. We think this work is extremely valuable, but we believe there is comparative advantage in focusing on the direct work, and that a small number of force multipliers can produce the same (if not larger) multiplicative effect as current efforts.</span><br /></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wiFv6LguphSxkzAnb/59b9b01e5e7beba73e7445a0236bb3e30810135eba52f412158e67834bc51af6/xdydnbww2loj5krzqfv6" /><p><br /><span>Harshul: In </span><i><span>Balatro</span></i><span>, your final score is calculated as </span><b><span>the number of chips you score </span></b><span>(blue) </span><b><span>times your mult</span></b><span> (red). I’m skeptical of a mult-heavy build (focusing on auxiliary work) without a strong base of chips to actually multiply (direct safety work). I’m more bought into a chip-heavy build (focusing on direct work, which I believe benefits more from added effort) alongside a smaller fraction of the field providing sufficient mult (auxiliary support).</span></p><h2><b><span>Deferring thinking to others</span></b></h2><p><span>Our subfield includes a multitude of conflicting agendas: whether or not alignment research is “good” to do, how much or little we should be focusing on political advocacy, how effective comms work is and could be, etc. Such conflicts don’t yet have a resolution. But, in order to “do things”, people will defer to a respected figure as opposed to building their own reasoning for their models. They will defer on what things are good and bad as a way to consolidate their consciousness and start doing things to “make impact”.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 52.26586102719033%;"><img alt="nize.webp" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786895652/lexical_client_uploads/zczavzan4ncpqd6dq272.webp" /></figure><p><span>As an example, upcoming technical researchers will often defer to what is popular as a proxy for impact. They may look at places like Redwood or Anthropic, and attest to wanting to work there to “do the most impactful work”. But in reality, it's very much up for debate as to whether a technical researcher should be going to said places in order to produce counterfactual impact (not to mention whether they should be doing technical research at all). But they instead defer to the vibes/popularity these places hold and conflate this with prospective impact.</span></p><h2><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nwpyhyagpPYDn4dAW/the-field-of-ai-alignment-a-postmortem-and-what-to-do-about"><b><span>Streetlighting</span></b></a></h2><p><span>AI safety is hard, but not everyone ends up working on the hardest problems. Instead, people focus on more tractable problems that may </span><i><span>seem</span></i><span> nearly as impactful, but actually fail solving the true bottlenecks at hand. We believe this arises through some combination of people getting turned away from working on hard problems after getting stuck, and people prioritizing fast execution over thinking deeply about their work. When pushed to “just do things”, it's often easiest to “just do” the easiest things.</span></p><p><span>(</span><i><span>Note</span></i><span>: Hard problems aren’t impactful by default, and easy problems aren’t auxiliary by default either. This section applies to the set of hard problems that are actually useful to work on.)</span></p><h2><b><span>How to avoid these:</span></b></h2><ul><li value="1"><span>Build your models</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Pursue the fundamentals: Read early AI Safety/alignment work, sequences, etc. </span></li><li value="2"><span>Read more modern work, stay up to date on meaningful new literature</span></li><li value="3"><span>Reflect on your reading regularly. Analyze when you update and what uncertainties you have after reading a piece.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Construct feedback loops</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Seek lots of feedback on your direction/work from different demographics, especially those with conflicting views.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Work in public and let others help you course correct</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Try and maintain strong epistemic, and avoid subtle dishonesties</span></li></ul><p><span>We do not claim to have it all figured out ourselves. We have not thought as deeply as many others, and this post is not meant to be a “call out” of people who have exhibited the aforementioned behaviors. Rather, this is a call for improvement, and a raise in standards. We do not have</span><i><span> </span></i><span>to sacrifice the understanding of our problems, haphazardly defer, or avoid what is difficult. We </span><b><span>can</span></b><span> try harder, be smarter, and raise the bar of the work we devote our time to.</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wiFv6LguphSxkzAnb/psa-we-can-do-better#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The American People Really Hate Data Centers](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EDKw7KyonrvskqZ7o/the-american-people-really-hate-data-centers)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>There are at least five different core questions around data centers and their politics.</p>
<ol>
<li>In what ways are specific concerns people raise about data centers legitimate?</li>
<li>In what ways are specific concerns people raise about generative AI legitimate?</li>
<li>Is it in general a good idea to build more data centers?</li>
<li>How can we get America to build more (or less) data centers in a better way?</li>
<li>Why do the American people increasingly really, really hate data centers?</li>
</ol>
<p>This post focuses on question five, the latest in a series of such posts <a href="https://jasmi.news/p/no-data-centers-in-my-backyard">most famously Jasmine Sun</a>’s road trip.</p>
<p>It is mostly not about the first four questions.</p>

</div>

<h4>The American People Really Hate Data Centers</h4>


<p>Support for data centers keeps cratering. <a href="https://x.com/pitdesi/status/2090497537199464559">Americans really</a>, <a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2090589885199769841">really</a> <a href="https://heatmap.news/daily/data-center-opposition-poll-collapse">hate data centers</a>, and 75% oppose local data center development, despite the economic benefits.</p>
<p><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-211797956">Cremieux illustrates that this opposition</a> mostly does not get solved via arguments about the tangible benefits or costs of data centers. People don’t believe you, and even when they do believe you they don’t care because that wasn’t their true objection. Persuasion buys you a few percent support, but from new very high levels of opposition.</p>
